She usually just says my name, or... or makes a very short comment about whatever I happen to be doing right then. \n\nIt's just... it's so clear that I've actually answered her out loud a few times before realizing there's absolutely no one in the room with me. It's probably just an echo in my mind from being around chatting teenagers all day, don't you think? I hope that doesn't sound too strange...\n\nDoctor: Do you believe the coded messages in the emails and announcements are actually intended for you, or is it just a feeling you can't shake?\n\nPatient: Oh, that's a very fair question. I... I really want to say it's just a silly feeling. I try so hard to logic my way out of it, you know? Because when my colleagues are being so patient and they gently show me on their screens that the emails were clearly sent to everyone on the staff... I see it. I really do. \n\nBut... oh, I feel so foolish admitting this. Even when I see the staff list right there, I just can't shake the feeling that the words themselves are actually coded messages about me. It’s like there's a hidden meaning in the text, or in the way the morning announcements are phrased, that only I'm supposed to understand. \n\nI know it sounds completely irrational when I say it to a professional like yourself, and I'm really trying not to let it affect my work. I just... I can't seem to turn the belief off, no matter how much proof I'm given. I hope you don't think I'm being too ridiculous?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to organize your thoughts or express them clearly to others?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I wouldn't say so. I mean, I'm a teacher, so I suppose communicating clearly is quite literally my job! I'm always up in front of the classroom, explaining history lessons and giving instructions. \n\nSometimes, if I'm very tired after a long day, I might lose my train of thought for a moment, but... well, that's perfectly normal for anyone my age, isn't it? I certainly don't think I'm confusing anyone or having trouble putting my sentences together. I hope I've been making sense to you today? I really do try my best to be clear.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your behavior that are out of the ordinary for you?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... changes in behavior? Well, I try to keep everything very professional at school, as you can imagine. I wouldn't say there's anything terribly major... just some silly little habits that have popped up recently. I'm sure it's just from the stress of the term, right? \n\nBut... well, I have noticed that sometimes during my lessons, or when we're having our art-fair committee meetings, I'll just... stop what I'm doing. Right in the middle of a task. And I'll find myself rearranging the whiteboard markers, or the stacks of papers on the desk, into a very, very particular pattern. \n\nAnd... oh, this is a bit embarrassing to admit, but sometimes instead of answering a colleague or a student normally, I'll just repeat the phrase they just said right back to them. \n\nIt interrupts what I’m supposed to be doing, and I always feel so terribly embarrassed when I catch myself doing it. I just quickly smile and try to move on. It's really just a little quirk from being a bit overworked and distracted, don't you think? I hope you don't think it means I'm not doing a good job with the students... I really do try to keep things running smoothly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the woman's voice you hear is real?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... that's a bit tricky to answer. I... I really want to assure you that I know there isn't actually a physical person standing there. I'm a sensible woman, after all. But... oh, it's just so embarrassing to talk about. In the exact moment it happens, it really does sound completely real. \n\nSometimes, when I'm just in the quiet of my house, or right after the classroom empties out for the day, I'll hear her. She usually just says my name, or makes a very short little comment about whatever I happen to be doing, like organizing my desk. And it sounds so perfectly clear that... well, I actually answer her back out loud before I even realize no one is there. \n\nOnce I look around and see the room is completely empty, I know it wasn't real. It's just my mind playing a silly trick on me when it's a bit too quiet, don't you think? I certainly hope I'm not coming across as completely unhinged to you... I really am quite grounded most of the time!\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your belief about the coded messages is not true?\n\nPatient: Oh... well, yes, I suppose it's entirely possible. I really do try to look at things logically. But... oh, this is actually sort of why I came in to see you in the first place, to be perfectly honest. It feels a bit silly to admit, but... well, I just haven't been able to shake this feeling lately that certain staff emails, or even the morning announcements over the loudspeaker, are actually coded messages meant specifically for me. \n\nAnd, you know, my colleagues have been so lovely about it. When I've mentioned it, they very gently show me the email list and point out that the messages were sent to everyone on staff. And when they do that, I can see the proof right in front of me. I really do know they're right. \n\nBut... for some reason, that feeling just lingers, even when they show me. It's probably just a bit of an overactive imagination on my part, don't you think? Maybe from teaching too many history lessons about wartime codes! I do try my absolute best to be rational about it, and I never let it disrupt the school day. I hope I don't sound completely foolish to you? I really am trying to keep things in perspective.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having trouble staying on topic in your conversations?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... staying on topic? No, I really don't think so. I mean, as a teacher, I have to be quite organized with my thoughts to get through a lesson plan, as you can imagine. \n\nI suppose anyone might go off on a tiny tangent now and then if they're just chatting with a friend over tea, especially after a long week of grading, but... no, I certainly wouldn't say I have any real trouble with it. I always try very hard to be a good listener and keep things clear when I'm speaking with colleagues or the students. \n\nI... I hope I've been making sense to you today? I really do try my absolute best to be straightforward and answer everything properly. I certainly wouldn't want to waste your time by rambling!\n\nDoctor: Do you believe the coded messages are coming from a specific person or group?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... as I was mentioning a moment ago about those staff emails and the morning announcements... I really don't know exactly *who* would be sending them. I certainly don't want you to think I believe there's some sort of... oh, I don't know, organized conspiracy or a specific group out to get me. That would sound terribly paranoid, wouldn't it? And I know I'm a perfectly reasonable person. \n\nIt's just... whoever writes the daily bulletins or sends out the administration emails... even when my colleagues are so sweet and gently show me the distribution list to prove the messages went out to absolutely everyone, I just have this unshakeable feeling that the words are actually coded specifically about me. \n\nI'm so sorry to bring it up again, I know it sounds completely absurd when I say it out loud. I really don't think anyone at the school wishes me ill. I hope you don't think I'm making a mountain out of a molehill with all this? I really am trying my best to look at it rationally.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having difficulty expressing your thoughts in a way that makes sense to others?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I wouldn't say that at all. I mean, as I mentioned, being a teacher, communication is really the cornerstone of what I do. I have to explain complex historical events to teenagers every day, so I think I'm quite practiced at making sure my thoughts are clear and easy to understand. \n\nI suppose everyone has moments where they might stumble over a word or not phrase something perfectly the first time, especially if they're tired, but... no, I really don't think people have a hard time understanding me. My students always seem to follow along just fine, and my colleagues have never mentioned anything of the sort. \n\nI do hope I've been clear with you today? I really want to make sure I'm giving you the right information and not confusing things. I'd hate to think I wasn't making sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself jumping from one idea to another unrelated idea when speaking or thinking?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. I really don't think I do that. I mean, perhaps if I'm very excited about a new topic in history class, I might get a little enthusiastic and bring in a few different historical parallels, but it all connects back to the lesson, I promise. \n\nI really try to be quite deliberate when I speak. I know how important it is to be logical and keep a straight line of thought, especially when you're trying to set a good example for the students. I certainly wouldn't want anyone to think my mind is just... bouncing all over the place. That wouldn't be very professional, would it?\n\nI think I've been keeping on track with you, haven't I? I really do want to make sure I'm answering your questions properly and not just going off on tangents. I'm doing all right with that, aren't I?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having thoughts that don't seem to make sense or are difficult to understand?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. I really don't think so. I mean... my thoughts are always quite clear to me. I suppose everyone has moments where they might be a little fatigued after a long day of grading papers, and maybe things get just a tiny bit fuzzy then, but... no, I certainly don't have thoughts that are nonsensical or confusing. \n\nI try very hard to keep my mind sharp. I read quite a bit, and I like to think I have a very organized, rational way of looking at the world. I really wouldn't want you to think my mind was slipping or anything like that. That would be dreadful, especially in my line of work. \n\nDo you think my thought process seems normal so far? I really am trying to be as helpful and accurate as I can be with all of this. I just hope I'm giving you the right kind of answers.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself having thoughts that seem to interfere with each other?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I wouldn't say they interfere with each other. I mean, sometimes when I'm planning a lesson and I have three different historical periods running through my head, it can get a bit... busy, I suppose. But that's just normal multitasking, isn't it? \n\nI really try to keep things orderly up there. I use lists and outlines, and I think I manage to keep everything quite separated and neat. I certainly wouldn't want you to think my mind is a jumble or that I can't keep my thoughts straight. I'm very careful about that. \n\nI hope I'm explaining this well enough? I just want to be sure I'm giving you what you need and not sounding like I'm disorganized. I'm doing okay, aren't I?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself having trouble putting your thoughts into words?\n\nPatient: I don't believe so, no. Communication is... well, it's really the core of what I do. You know, spending all day explaining history to teenagers, you simply have to be articulate. I suppose if it's the end of the term and I'm dreadfully tired, I might pause for a moment to find the perfect adjective, but... that's just normal fatigue, isn't it? Everyone does that. \n\nI think I've been expressing myself clearly to you today. You haven't noticed me struggling to find my words, have you? I really do hope I've been making perfect sense so far.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself losing your train of thought in the middle of conversations?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens, no. I... I really don't think so. I mean, occasionally in the classroom, if a student interrupts or drops a heavy textbook while I'm in the middle of a lecture, I might pause for a second to address it. But that's just... well, that's just managing a room, isn't it? I always pick right back up exactly where I left off. \n\nI pride myself on being very attentive when I speak with people. It's so important to be polite and present in a conversation. \n\nI haven't done that with you today, have I? I feel like I've been following our discussion quite well, but... you'd tell me if I was trailing off, wouldn't you? I really do want to make sure I'm giving you good answers and being as helpful as possible.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having thoughts that are not logically connected?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. I... I really try to be as logical as possible in everything I do. I mean, teaching history is all about cause and effect, isn't it? A leads to B, which leads to C. You simply can't afford to have disconnected thoughts if you want the students to understand the material.\n\nI suppose sometimes when I'm brainstorming a new way to present a topic, my mind might jump around a little bit... thinking of a map here, or a primary source document there. But there's always a clear thread connecting it all. It's just creative planning, really. \n\nI haven't said anything today that didn't make sense, have I? I always try to think things through before I speak, so I do hope I'm coming across as perfectly reasonable. I'm doing alright, aren't I?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having thoughts that are difficult for others to follow?\n\nPatient: Oh, I certainly hope not. I... I don't think so. As a teacher, my whole job is making sure people can follow what I'm saying. If my students couldn't understand my thoughts, I wouldn't be very good at what I do, would I? \n\nI mean, occasionally if I'm explaining something quite complex—like the intricate political alliances before the First World War—a student might ask me to clarify a point or go over it again. But that's just the material being a bit dense, not my thoughts being tangled or strange. I always try to lay things out very plainly for people.\n\nI hope I've been easy to follow during our chat today? You haven't found me confusing at all, have you? I'd feel just awful if I was making your job difficult. I really do want to be as clear as possible for you.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having thoughts that seem to loop back on themselves or repeat in a way that doesn't make sense?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I wouldn't say that. I mean... sometimes if I have a lot on my plate, like grading essays or preparing for parent-teacher conferences, I might go over my to-do list a few times in my head just to make sure I haven't forgotten anything important. But that's just being thorough, isn't it? \n\nI certainly don't get stuck in... in loops that don't make sense. I've always prided myself on having a very organized mind. You have to, really, to keep a classroom running smoothly. \n\nI haven't been repeating myself with you, have I? I feel like I've been giving you straightforward answers, but... please do let me know if I'm not giving you what you need. I want to be sure I'm doing this right.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself saying things that don't make sense to others?\n\nPatient: Oh, my... no, I really don't think so. I mean, I certainly try my best to be perfectly clear. \n\nSometimes, if I'm very passionate about a subject—say, the Industrial Revolution—I might speak a little quickly, or get a bit wrapped up in the details. A student might give me a slightly puzzled look, and I'll have to pause and say, \"Let me rephrase that.\" But that's just normal classroom communication, isn't it? It's certainly not that I'm saying things that are... nonsensical. \n\nI would hate to think I'm walking around confusing people. I haven't said anything odd today, have I? I really am trying to answer your questions as best I can. I hope I'm being helpful.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
